\chapter{Monopolarity}
\label{cap:mono}

\section{Previous results}
\label{sec:previous}

\begin{proposition}
    Let $G$ and $H$ be graphs.   If $G$ and $H$ are monopolar, then $G+H$ is
    monopolar.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $(A_1, B_1)$ and $(A_2, B_2)$ be the monopolar partitions of $G$ and
    $H$, respectively.   Since $A_1$ and $A_2$ are independent, we have that
    $A_1 \cup A_2$ is also independent.   Analogously, $G[B_1]$ and $G[B_2]$ are
    clusters, so their union is also a cluster.   Hence, $(A_1 \cup A_2, B_1
    \cup B_2)$ is a monopolar partition of $G+H$.
    
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    A graph is monopolar if and only if each of its connected components is
    monopolar.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}
    Every minimal obstruction for monopolarity is a connected graph.
\end{corollary}

We begin with the following auxiliary lemma that will help us prove that the
graphs in \Cref{fig:chordal-p5-free-obstructions} are indeed monopolar
obstructions.

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:monopolar_diamond}
    Let $G$ be a graph and suppose it has an induced copy of the diamond. Then,
    any monopolar partition of $G$ must be such that the chord of the diamond is
    in $B$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let $\p{a,b,c,d,a}$ be a $C_4$ in $G$ such that $a$ is adjacent to $c$ and
    $b$ is not adjacent to $d$. Suppose $G$ is monopolar and $(A,B)$ is a
    monopolar partition of $G$. If $a\in A$, it follows from $A$ being stable
    that $\p{b,c,d}$ is a $P_3$ in $B$, which contradicts that $G[B]$ is a
    cluster. Thus, $a\in B$, and similarly follows that $c\in B$.

\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{prop:minimal_obs_chordal}
    The graphs depicted in \Cref{fig:chordal-p5-free-obstructions} are minimal
    monopolar obstructions.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Consider the following labelling of $H_1$ (see \Cref{fig:labelling_h1}).
    \begin{figure}[htb!]
        \centering
        \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style = {vertex}, edge, node distance=1cm, on grid, label distance = 1.5pt]
            \node (a) [label={left:$a$}] {};
            \node (b) [right= of a, label={right:$b$}] {};
            \node (c) [below= of a, label={left:$c$}] {};
            \node (d) [right= of c, label={right:$d$}] {};
            \node (e) [below= of c, label={left:$e$}] {};
            \node (f) [below= of d, label={right:$f$}] {};

            \draw (a) to (b);
            \draw (a) to (c);
            \draw (a) to (d);
            \draw (b) to (c);
            \draw (b) to (d);
            \draw (c) to (d);
            \draw (c) to (e);
            \draw (c) to (f);
            \draw (d) to (e);
            \draw (d) to (f);
            \draw (e) to (f);
        \end{tikzpicture}
        \caption{A labelling of $H_1$.}
        \label{fig:labelling_h1}
    \end{figure}
    Suppose $H_1$ is monopolar. Notice that $cd$ is a chord of the copy of the
    diamond induced by $\l{a,c,d,e}$. Then, by \Cref{lem:monopolar_diamond}, $c$
    and $d$ are in $B$. Since $\l{a,b}$ is anticomplete to $\l{e,f}$, it follows
    that one of such sets must be contained in $A$ so that $B$ is $P_3$-free.
    This contradicts the fact that $A$ is stable. Therefore, $H_1$ is not
    monoplar, and in \Cref{fig:minimal_h1} the monopolar partitions of $H_1-v$
    are shown, just accounting for the non-isomorphic cases.

    Consider the following labelling of $H_4$ (see \Cref{fig:labelling_h4}).
    \begin{figure}[htb!]
        \centering
        \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node
            distance=1cm, on grid, edge, label distance = 1.5pt]
            \node (a) [label={above:$a$}] {};
            \node (b) [below right= of a, label={right:$b$}] {};
            \node (c) [below left= of a, label={left:$c$}] {};
            \node (d) [below left= of b, label={right:$d$}] {};
            \node (e) at ($(d) + (240:1)$) [label={left:$e$}] {};
            \node (f) at ($(d) + (300:1)$) [label={right:$f$}] {};
            \draw (a) to (b);
            \draw (a) to (c);
            \draw (a) to (d);
            \draw (b) to (d);
            \draw (c) to (d);
            \draw (d) to (f);
            \draw (d) to (e);
            \draw (e) to (f);
        \end{tikzpicture}
        \caption{A labelling of $H_4$.}
        \label{fig:labelling_h4}
    \end{figure}
    Notice $ad$ is a chord edge of a diamond in $H_4$. Since $e$ and $f$ are not
    adjacent to $a$, it follows that both must be in $A$ so $B$ is $P_3$-free,
    contracting that $A$ is stable. Therefore, $H_4$ is not monopolar, and in
    \Cref{fig:minimal_h4} are shown the monopolar partitions of $H_4 - v$.

                        \draw (c) to (d);
                        \draw (d) to (e);
                        \draw (d) to (f);

    once more that it is stable. Hence, $H_5$ is not monopolar, and in
    \Cref{fig:minimal_h5} the monopolar partitions of $H_5-v$ are shown.

Recall that split graphs are those whose vertex set can be partitioned into a
stable set and a clique. In particular, split graphs are monopolar. Since split
graphs are exactly the $\l{2K_2, C_4, C_5}$-free graphs, it follows that a
chordal graph is split if and only if it is $2K_2$-free. In
\cite{heggernesNJC14,hellDAM141}, linear-time certifying algorithms for
recognizing split graphs are shown. Thus, given a chordal graph, one can run
such an algorithm to decide whether the graph is split, which in turn implies
the graph is monopolar, or get a $2K_2$ as a certificate of non-membership.
Therefore, to solve \textsc{Monopolarity} in chordal graphs, it suffices to
solve it for those graphs that contain an induced copy of $2K_2$. 

Throughout the rest of this section, we assume that the graphs have an induced
copy of $2K_2$, whose vertex set is denoted by $Z=\l{a,b,c,d}$ and it is such
that $ab$ and $cd$ are the disjoint edges of the $2K_2$. The following lemma
asserts there are two types of $P_5$-free graphs with an induced copy of $2K_2$.

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:chordal_connectedness}
    Let $G$ be a $P_5$-free graph with an induced copy of a $2K_2$, $G[Z]$. One
    of the following must be true:
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item There is a vertex adjacent to both edges of $G[Z]$.
        \item There are adjacent vertices $x$ and $y$ with $x$ complete to the
        edge $ab$ and anticomplete to the edge $cd$, whereas $y$ is complete to
        $cd$ and anticomplete to $ab$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose (i) does not happen. Since $G$ is $P_5$-free, there exists a path of
    minimum length, $P$, whose initial vertex is in $\l{a,b}$, its final vertex
    is in $\l{c,d}$, and all middle vertices are not in $Z$. Since (i) is not
    true, such a path has at least 4 vertices. Moreover, if it has at least $5$
    vertices, it follows that $P$ contains an copy of $P_5$, which is induced
    since $P$ is of minimum length.

    Therefore, suppose without loss of generality that there are adjacent
    vertices $x$ and $y$ such that $P=\p{a,x,y,c}$. Since $P$ is of minimum
    length, $x$ is anticomplete to $\l{c,d}$ and $y$ is anticomplete to
    $\l{a,b}$. Moreover, if $x$ is not adjacent to $b$, notice $\p{b,a,x,y,c}$
    is an induced copy of $P_5$ (see \Cref{fig:chordal_connectedness}).

    \begin{figure}[htb!]
        \centering
        \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
            on grid, edge]
            \node[Label] (z) {};
            \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
            \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
            \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
            \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (d) {};
            \node (x) at (0,0.5) {};
            \node (y) at (0,-0.5){};

            \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
            \draw (a) to (b);
            \draw (x) to (a);
            \draw (y) to (c);
            \draw (x) to (y);
        \end{tikzpicture}
        \caption{An induced copy of $P_5$ given that $x$ is not adjacent to $b$.}
        \label{fig:chordal_connectedness}
    \end{figure}
    
    Thus, $x$ is adjacent to $b$. Similarly follows that $y$ is adjacent to $d$.
    Hence, $x$ is complete to $\l{a,b}$ and $y$ is complete to $\l{c,d}$.

\end{proof}

The next lemmas describe the structure of the exact neighborhoods of subsets of
$Z$ given that a graph is free of the minimal obstructions given in
\Cref{fig:chordal-p5-free-obstructions}. For simplicity, throughout the chapter,
exact neighborhoods $N_X$ when $X$ is of size one will be called 1-bags.
Similarly, sets $N_X$ when $X$ is of size two and three will be called 2-bags
and 3-bags, respectively.

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:neighborhoods_chordal_anti}
    Let $G$ be a connected chordal $P_5$-free graph with and induced copy of
    $2K_2$ and free of the graphs depicted in
    \Cref{fig:chordal-p5-free-obstructions}. Let $X$ and $Y$ be different proper
    subsets of $Z$.
    \begin{lemenum}
        \item If $N_X$ and $N_Y$ are 1-bags adjacent to the same edge of the
            $2K_2$, then $N_X$ is anticomplete to $N_Y$.
            \label{lem:chordal_anti_2}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 1-bag, then $N_X$ is anticomplete to $N_Z$.
            \label{lem:chordal_anti_1}
    \end{lemenum}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    \leavevmode \par
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a}$ and $Y=\l{b}$.
            If $x\in N_X$ is adjacent to $y\in N_Y$, then $\p{a,b,y,x,a}$
            induces a copy of $C_4$ (see \cref{fig:chordal_anti_1}). Therefore,
            $N_X$ is anticomplete to $N_Y$.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \node[Label] (z) {};
                    \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                    \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                    \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (c) {};
                    \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (d) {};
                    \node[above=1.5 of a] (x)  {};
                    \node[above=1.5 of b] (y) {};

                    \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
                    \draw (a) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (a);
                    \draw (y) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (y);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_4$ given that $N_X$ is not anticomplete to $N_Y$.}
                \label{fig:chordal_anti_1}
            \end{figure}

        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a}$. If $x\in N_X$
            is adjacent to $z\in N_Z$, then $\l{a,b,c,d,x,z}$ induces a copy of
            $H_4$ (see \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_2}). Hence, $N_X$ is anticomplete
            to $N_Z$.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \node (z) {};
                    \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                    \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                    \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                    \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                    \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};

                    \draw (c) to (d);
                    \draw (a) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (a);
                    \draw (z) to (a);
                    \draw (z) to (b);
                    \draw (z) to (c);
                    \draw (z) to (d);
                    \draw (x) to (z);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $H_4$ given that $N_X$ is not anticomplete to $N_Z$.}
                \label{fig:chordal_anti_2}
            \end{figure}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:neighborhoods_chordal_types}
    Let $G$ be a connected chordal $P_5$-free graph with and induced copy of
    $2K_2$ and free of the graphs depicted in
    \Cref{fig:chordal-p5-free-obstructions}. Let $X$ and $Y$ be different
    subsets of $Z$.
    \begin{lemenum}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 2-bag adjacent to both edges of the $2K_2$,
            then $N_X$ is empty. \label{lem:chordal_types_2}
        \item If $N_X$ is a bag adjacent to both edges of the $2K_2$, then $N_X$
            is a clique. \label{lem:chordal_types_clique_bag}
        \item The exact neighborhood $N_Z$ has cardinality at most one.
            \label{lem:chordal_types_1}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 2-bag complete to an edge of the $2K_2$, then
            $G[N_X]$ is split. \label{lem:chordal_types_6}
        \item At most one bag is adjacent to both edges of the $2K_2$.
            \label{lem:chordal_types_3}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 3-bag and $N_Y$ is the complementary 1-bag, then at
            least one of $N_X$ and $N_Y$ is empty. \label{lem:chordal_types_pending}
    \end{lemenum}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    \leavevmode\par
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,c}$. If $x\in
            N_X$, notice $\p{b,a,x,c,d}$ is an induced copy of $P_5$ (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_types_2}). It follows that $N_X$ is empty.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \node (z) {};
                    \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                    \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                    \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                    \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};

                    \draw (c) to (d);
                    \draw (a) to (b);
                    \draw (z) to (a);
                    \draw (z) to (c);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $P_5$ given that $N_X$ is nonempty.}
                \label{fig:chordal_types_2}
            \end{figure}
        \item Suppose that $x$ and $y$ are nonadjacent vertices in $N_X$. By
            (i), it follows that $N_X$ is a 3-bag or $X=Z$. Suppose without loss
            of generality that $\l{a,b,c}\subseteq X$. Observe $\p{a,x,c,y,a}$
            is an induced copy of $C_4$ (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_types_clique_bag}). Therefore, $N_X$ is a clique.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \node[Label] (z) {};
                    \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                    \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                    \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                    \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (d) {};
                    \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                    \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};

                    \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
                    \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                    \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                    \draw[lightgray] (y) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (a);
                    \draw (x) to (c);
                    \draw (y) to (a);
                    \draw (y) to (c);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_4$ given that $N_X$ is not a clique.}
                \label{fig:chordal_types_clique_bag}
            \end{figure}
        \item Suppose $x$ and $y$ are vertices in $N_Z$. By (ii), $x$ and $y$
            are adjacent. Notice that $\l{a,b,c,d,x,y}$ induces a copy of $H_1$
            (see \Cref{fig:chordal_types_1}). Thus, $N_Z$ has cardinality at
            most one.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \node[Label] (z) {};
                    \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                    \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                    \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                    \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                    \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                    \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};

                    \draw (c) to (d);
                    \draw (a) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (b);
                    \draw (y) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (d);
                    \draw (y) to (d);
                    \draw (x) to (a);
                    \draw (x) to (c);
                    \draw (y) to (a);
                    \draw (y) to (c);
                    \draw (x) to (y);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $H_1$ given that $N_Z$ has more than one vertex.}
                \label{fig:chordal_types_1}
            \end{figure}
        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,b}$. If $G[N_X]$
            is not split, since $G$ is chordal, it follows that $G[N_X]$
            contains an induced copy of $2K_2$. Then, adjacent vertices $a$ and
            $b$ are complete to the copy of $2K_2$ in $N_X$, which contradicts
            (iii). Hence, $G[N_X]$ is split.
        \item Let $N_X$ and $N_Y$ be nonempty different bags adjacent to both
            edges of the $2K_2$. By (i), both $X$ and $Y$ have at least three
            vertices in $Z$. Suppose without loss of generality that
            $\l{a,b,c}\subseteq X$ and $b\in Y$. Let $x\in N_X$ and $y\in N_Y$.

            If one of $X$ and $Y$ is equal to $Z$, suppose without loss of
            generality that $X=Z$. Since $X\neq Y$, suppose without loss of
            generality that $c\notin Y$. Since $N_Y$ is a 3-bag adajcent to both
            edges of the $2K_2$, it follows that $Y=\l{a,b,d}$. Notice either
            $\l{a,b,c,d,x,y}$ induces a copy of $H_2$, or $\p{a,x,d,y,a}$ is a
            $C_4$, depending on whether $x$ is adjacent to $y$ or not,
            respectively (see \Cref{fig:chordal_types_3_1}).

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \begin{scope}[xshift=-2cm]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw (c) to (d);
                        \draw (a) to (b);
                        \draw (x) to (b);
                        \draw (y) to (b);
                        \draw (x) to (d);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (a);
                        \draw (x) to (c);
                        \draw (y) to (a);
                        \draw (x) to (y);
                    \end{scope}

                    \begin{scope}[xshift=2cm]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (y) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (c);
                        \draw (x) to (d);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (a);
                        \draw (y) to (a);
                    \end{scope}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_4$ or $H_2$ given that both $N_X$ 
                    and $N_Y$ are nonempty.}
                \label{fig:chordal_types_3_1}
            \end{figure}

            If neither $X$ nor $Y$ is $Z$, then $X=\l{a,b,c}$. We claim that $y$
            is adjacent to $x$ and $c$. Indeed, since $Y$ is adjacent to the
            edge $cd$, $y$ is adjacent to at least one between $c$ and $d$. If
            $y$ is adjacent to $d$, then $y$ must be adjacent to both $x$ and
            $c$, otherwise, there is an induced copy of $C_4$ or $C_5$ (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_types_3_2}).

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \begin{scope}[xshift=-3cm]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (a);
                        \draw (c) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (b);
                        \draw (y) to (b);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (c);
                    \end{scope}

                    \begin{scope}[]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (a);
                        \draw[lightgray] (y) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (b);
                        \draw (y) to (b);
                        \draw (x) to (c);
                        \draw (y) to (c);
                    \end{scope}

                    \begin{scope}[xshift=3cm]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (a);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (y) to (b);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (c) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (c);
                        \draw (y) to (x);
                    \end{scope}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_4$ or $C_5$ given that $y$ is not
                complete to $\l{x,c}$.}
                \label{fig:chordal_types_3_2}
            \end{figure}

            This last argument also implies that if $y$ is adjacent to $c$, then
            $y$ is adjacent to $x$. At any rate, since $y$ is adjacent to at
            least one between $c$ and $d$, it follows that $y$ is adjacent to
            both $x$ and $c$. Moreover, since $Y$ is different from $X$ and $Z$,
            it follows that $Y=\l{b,c,d}$, so $y$ is adjacent to $d$. Thus,
            $\l{a,b,c,d,x,y}$ induces a copy of $H_3$ (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_type_3_3}). At last, we conclude that one of $N_X$
            and $N_Y$ must be empty.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \node[Label] (z) {};
                    \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                    \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (b) {};
                    \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                    \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                    \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                    \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};

                    \draw (a) to (b);
                    \draw (x) to (a);
                    \draw (x) to (b);
                    \draw (y) to (b);
                    \draw (y) to (d);
                    \draw (c) to (d);
                    \draw (x) to (c);
                    \draw (y) to (x);
                    \draw (y) to (c);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $H_3$ given that $N_X$ and $N_Y$ are both nonempty.}
                \label{fig:chordal_type_3_3}
            \end{figure}
        
        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,b,c}$, so
            $Y=\l{d}$. If $x\in N_X$ and $y\in N_Y$, then either $\p{x,c,d,y,x}$ is
            a $C_4$, or $\p{a,x,c,d,y}$ is a $P_5$, depending on wheter $x$ is
            adjacent to $y$ or not (see \Cref{fig:chordal_types_pending}). Hence,
            one between $N_X$ and $N_Y$ is empty.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \begin{scope}[xshift=-2cm]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (a);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (c) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (c);
                        \draw (y) to (x);
                    \end{scope}

                    \begin{scope}[xshift=2cm]
                        \node[Label] (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1cm and 0.75cm of z] (d) {};
                        \node[left=0.75 of z] (x)  {};
                        \node[right=0.75 of z] (y)  {};
    
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                        \draw (x) to (a);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (c) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (c);
                    \end{scope}
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_4$ or $P_5$ given that both $N_X$
                    and $N_Y$ are nonempty.}
                \label{fig:chordal_types_pending}
            \end{figure}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

As we will see, the proof of the main theorem will require us to check the case
when $N_Z$ is nonempty. In order to facilitate its proof, the next three lemmas
provide additional relations between the exact neighborhoods given that $N_Z$ is
nonempty. If that is the case, notice that, by \Cref{lem:chordal_types_1}, there
is just one vertex in $N_Z$, namely $z$.

\begin{lemma}
    Let $G$ be a connected chordal $P_5$-free graph with and induced copy of
    $2K_2$ and free of the graphs depicted in
    \Cref{fig:chordal-p5-free-obstructions}. Let $X$ and $Y$ be different proper
    subsets of $Z$. Suppose that $N_Z=\l{z}$.
    \begin{lemenum}
        \item If both $N_X$ and $N_Y$ are 2-bags complete to different edges of
            the $2K_2$, then $N_X$ is anticomplete to $N_Y$.
            \label{lem:chordal_anti_3}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 2-bag complete to an edge of the $2K_2$, then $N_X$
            is anticomplete to $N_\varnothing \cap N(z)$.
            \label{lem:chordal_anti_2_bag}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 2-bag complete to an edge of the $2K_2$, then
            $N_\varnothing$ is anticomplete to $N_X \setminus N(z)$.
            \label{lem:chordal_anti_5}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 2-bag complete to an edge of the $2K_2$, and $N_Y$
            is a 1-bag, then $N_Y$ is anticomplete to $N_X \setminus N(z)$.
            \label{lem:chordal_anti_2_bag_2}
        \item If $N_X$ is a 1-bag, then $N_X$ is anticomplete to
            $N_\varnothing$. \label{lem:chordal_anti_4}
    \end{lemenum}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    \leavevmode \par
    \begin{enumerate}[label= (\roman*)]
        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,b}$, so
            $Y=\l{c,d}$. If $x\in N_X$ is adjacent to $y\in N_Y$, then $G$
            contains an induced copy of $C_5$, $C_4$ or $H_2$, depending on
            whether $x$ and $y$ are adjacent to $z$ or not (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_3}). We conclude that $N_X$ is anticomplete
            to $N_Y$.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]
                \centering
                \begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={vertex}, node distance=1cm,
                    on grid, edge]
                    \begin{scope}[xshift=-3cm]
                        \node (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1 and 0.75 of z] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1 and 0.75 of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1 and 0.75 of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1 and 0.75 of z, lightgray] (d) {};
                        \node[above left=0.5 and 0.75 of z] (x) {};
                        \node[below left=0.5 and 0.75 of z] (y) {};
        
                        \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (z) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (z) to (d);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (y) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (a);
                        \draw (z) to (a);
                        \draw (y) to (c);
                        \draw (x) to (y);
                        \draw (z) to (c);
                    \end{scope}

                    \begin{scope}
                        \node (z) {};
                        \node[above left=1 and 0.75 of z, lightgray] (a) {};
                        \node[above right=1 and 0.75 of z, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1 and 0.75 of z] (c) {};
                        \node[below right=1 and 0.75 of z, lightgray] (d) {};
                        \node[above left=0.5 and 0.75 of z] (x) {};
                        \node[below left=0.5 and 0.75 of z] (y) {};

                        \draw[lightgray] (c) to (d);
                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (z) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (z) to (d);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (x) to (a);
                        \draw[lightgray] (z) to (a);
                        \draw[lightgray] (y) to (d);
                        \draw (x) to (z);
                        \draw (y) to (c);
                        \draw (x) to (y);
                        \draw (z) to (c);
                    \end{scope}

                        \draw (x) to (b);
                        \draw (x) to (a);
                        \draw (z) to (a);
                        \draw (y) to (d);
                        \draw (y) to (z);
                        \draw (x) to (z);
                        \draw (x) to (y);

                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_5$ ($\p{a,z,c,y,x,a}$), $C_4$

                that $N_X$ is not anticomplete to $N_Y$.}
                \label{fig:chordal_anti_3}
            \end{figure}

        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,b}$. If $x\in N_X$
            is adjacent to a vertex $w\in N_\varnothing \cap N(z)$, then $G$
            contains either an induced copy of $H_4$ or $C_4$, depending on
            whether $x$ is adjacent to $z$ or not, respectively (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_2_bag}). Hence, $N_X$ is anticomplete to
            $N_\varnothing \cap N(z)$.

        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,b}$. Let $x\in N_X

            $w$ is adjacent to $z$ or not, respectively (see
            \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_5}). Therefore, $N_\varnothing$ is
            anticomplete to $N_X \setminus N(z)$.

        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a,b}$. Suppose $y\in
            N_Y$ is adjacent to $x\in N_X \setminus N(z)$. By
            \Cref{lem:chordal_anti_1}, $y$ is not adjacent to $z$.
        
            On the one hand, if $N_Y$ is adjacent to the edge $ab$, suppose
            without loss of generality that $Y=\l{b}$. Then, $\p{y,x,a,z,c}$ is
            an induced copy of $P_5$ (see \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_2_bag_2_1}).

            \begin{figure}[htb!]

                    \draw[lightgray] (b) to (x);

                    \draw (w) to (x);
                    \draw (w) to (a);

                \caption{An induced copy of $P_5$ given that $N_Y$ is not

            On the other hand, if $N_Y$ is adjacent to the edge $cd$, suppose
            without loss of generality that $Y=\l{d}$. Then, $\p{x,a,z,d,y,x}$
            is an induced copy of $C_5$ (see \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_2_bag_2_2}).
            Thus, $N_Y$ is anticomplete to $N_X \setminus N(z)$.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]

                    \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);

                    \draw[lightgray] (w) to (b);
                    \draw[lightgray] (v) to (c);

                    \draw (d) to (x);
                    \draw (v) to (d);
                    \draw (v) to (a);
                    \draw (w) to (x);
                    \draw (w) to (a);
                \end{tikzpicture}
                \caption{An induced copy of $C_5$ given that $N_Y$ is not
                    anticomplete to $N_X\setminus N(z)$.}
                \label{fig:chordal_anti_2_bag_2_2}
            \end{figure}
        
        \item Suppose without loss of generality that $X=\l{a}$. By
            \Cref{lem:chordal_anti_1}, $x\in N_X$ is not adjacent to $z$. If
            $w\in N_\varnothing$ is adjacent to $x$, then $G$ contains an
            induced copy of $C_4$ or $P_5$, depending on whether $w$ is adjacent
            to $z$ or not, respectively (see \Cref{fig:chordal_anti_4}). Hence,
            $N_X$ is anticomplete to $N_\varnothing$.

            \begin{figure}[htb!]

                        \draw[lightgray] (a) to (b);
                        \draw[lightgray] (v) to (b);
                        \draw (v) to (a);
                        \draw (v) to (x);
                        \draw (w) to (x);
                        \draw (w) to (a);
                    \end{scope}

                    \begin{scope}[xshift=2cm]
                        \node (v) {};

                        \node[above right=1cm and 0.75cm of v, lightgray] (b) {};
                        \node[below left=1cm and 0.75cm of v] (c) {};

                        \draw[lightgray] (v) to (b);
